=== Codeine/Phenylephrine/Promethazine (Merck Manual Professional) ===
Source: https://www.merckmanuals.com/professional/drug-names-generic-and-brand (productid 995)

NURSING QUICK-REFERENCE SUMMARY
Auto-generated on 2026-09-25 from the prescribing information in this file; full details follow below.

Drug Class: Codeine, phenylephrine, and promethazine are used together in an oral preparation to treat upper respiratory congestion, cough, and related symptoms.

Routes: oral; Common adult doses: 30 mL/24 hours PO.

Common Side Effects (excluding routine GI upset): apnea, coma, cyanosis, dyspnea, pulmonary edema, respiratory arrest, respiratory depression, ileus, opioid-induced esophageal dysfunction, xerophthalmia, anxiety, confusion, dizziness, drowsiness, fatigue, hallucinations, headache, hypotension, impaired cognition, insomnia, palpitations, psychosis, restlessness, seizures, angina, bradycardia, heart failure, hypertension, intracranial bleeding, myocardial infarction

In an Overdose: Intracranial bleeding has been reported with sympathomimetic therapy; cerebral bleeding is likely due to associated drug-induced hypertension and has been reported following phenylephrine overdosage. In children less than 12 years, these drug products have been associated with fatal respiratory depression; postmarketing events seen in children less than 12 years of age include accidental overdose, bronchopneumonia, coma, cyanosis...

Key Nursing Considerations: Contraindications/precautions: NOTE: This monograph discusses the contraindications/precautions of codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine combination products.

Brand Names
  - Pentazine VC with Codeine
  - Prometh VC with Codeine

Dosage Forms
  - Codeine Phosphate 10mg/5mL, Phenylephrine Hydrochloride 5mg/5mL, Promethazine Hydrochloride 6.25mg/5mL Oral solution

Indication Specific Dosing
  General Information
  When discontinuing codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine therapy in individuals who are opioid-dependent, taper the dose gradually by 25% to 50% every 2 to 4 days. If the individual develops signs and symptoms of withdrawal, raise the dose to the previous level and taper more slowly by increasing the interval between doses, decreasing the amount of change in dose, or both.
  Indications
    Routes
      Heading
      Oral dosage (oral solutions with codeine 10 mg, phenylephrine 5 mg, and promethazine 6.25 mg per 5 mL)
      Route Age Groups
        Heading
        Adults
        Dosage Description
        5 mL PO every 4 to 6 hours as needed. Max: Do not exceed 30 mL per 24 hours. LIMITATION OF USE: Reserve codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine for use in adults for whom alternative treatment options are ineffective, not tolerated, or would be otherwise inadequate to provide sufficient management of cough. Prescribe for the shortest duration consistent with individual patient treatment goals. Monitor patients closely for respiratory depression, especially within the first 24 to 72 hours of initiating therapy; proper dosing is essential to reduce the risk of respiratory depression. Reevaluate patients with unresponsive cough in 5 days or sooner for possible underlying pathology, such as foreign body or lower respiratory tract disease. CONTINUED TREATMENT: If a patient requires a refill, reevaluate the cause of the cough and the need for continued treatment. DISCONTINUATION: Do not rapidly reduce the dose or abruptly discontinue this product in a physically-dependent patient; taper the dose gradually, by 25% to 50% every 2 to 4 days, while monitoring carefully for signs and symptoms of withdrawal. If withdrawal occurs, raise the dose to the previous level and taper more slowly, either by increasing the interval between decreases, decreasing the amount of change in dose, or both.
    Heading
    For the temporary relief of cough and upper respiratory symptoms (e.g., nasal congestion) associated with allergy or the common cold

Contraindications And Precautions
  Headline
  Contraindications/Warnings/Precautions
  General Information
  NOTE: This monograph discusses the contraindications/precautions of codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine combination products. Clinicians may wish to consult the individual monographs for more information about each agent.
 
Although true opiate agonist hypersensitivity is rare, patients who have demonstrated a prior hypersensitivity reaction to codeine or other opioid agonists of the phenanthrene subclass (i.e., morphine, oxycodone and hydromorphone) should not receive codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products.
 
Phenylephrine is absolutely contraindicated in patients with sulfite hypersensitivity unless the patient is being treated for an emergent condition such as anaphylaxis or cardiac arrest.
 
Promethazine is a phenothiazine-type antihistamine and is contraindicated for use in patients with known phenothiazine hypersensitivity. Cross-sensitivity may occur. This contraindication includes patients who have experienced agranulocytosis, bone marrow suppression, blood dyscrasias, or jaundice due to phenothiazine therapy.
 
Any patient receiving codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine combinations should be warned about the possibility of sedation and to use caution when driving or operating machinery until the effects of these drugs are known. Carefully supervise pediatric patients while they are riding bicycles or participating in activities that require mental alertness or physical coordination.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products should be used with caution in the elderly. Geriatric patients may be more susceptible to the potential cardiac, CNS, ocular, genitourinary, GI or respiratory effects of the drugs contained in this combination.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine should be avoided in patients with open-angle glaucoma or closed-angle glaucoma because of the anticholinergic effects of promethazine and the sympathomimetic effects of phenylephrine. Both drugs may induce increased intraocular pressure or may precipitate an acute attack of glaucoma or previously undiagnosed glaucoma. Other ocular effects resulting from the anticholinergic effects of promethazine include dry eyes or blurred vision. The opiate agonist codeine may cause changes in accommodation. Potential ocular effects of this drug combination may be of most significance in the elderly and in wearers of contact lenses.
 
Phenylephrine should be used with caution in men with symptomatic, benign prostatic hypertrophy due to the potential for urinary retention. Codeine can also cause urinary retention and oliguria, due to increasing the tension of the detrusor muscle, and promethazine has substantial anticholinergic effects. Because of anticholinergic actions, use promethazine with caution in patients with bladder obstruction, pelvic tumors, prostatic hypertrophy, renal disease, renal impairment or renal failure, urethral stricture, or urinary retention. The elderly are generally more susceptible to these genitourinary effects.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine should be used cautiously in patients with GI disease including GI obstruction or ileus, ulcerative colitis, or pre-existing constipation due to the effects of codeine and promethazine on the gastrointestinal tract. Opiate agonists may obscure the diagnosis or clinical course in patients with acute abdominal conditions. Codeine is contraindicated in patients who have or are suspected of having paralytic ileus. Patients with acute ulcerative colitis or other inflammatory bowel disease may be more sensitive to the constipating effects of opiate agonists. Although opiate agonists are contraindicated for use in patients with diarrhea secondary to poisoning or infectious diarrhea, antimotility agents have been used successfully in these patients. If possible, opiate agonists should not be given until the toxic substance has been eliminated.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products should be avoided in those with significant cerebrovascular disease. Phenylephrine is relatively contraindicated in patients with a history of cerebral arteriosclerosis, aneurysm, intracranial bleeding, stroke, or organic brain syndrome because of the sympathomimetic activity of the drug in the CNS and the potential for cerebrovascular hemorrhage. In addition, patients with head trauma or with increased intracranial pressure should be given promethazine with caution, because promethazine can make it difficult to evaluate neurologic parameters. Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine use is contraindicated in patients in a coma or in a comatose state.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products should be avoided in patients with significant cardiac disease. Phenylephrine exerts detrimental effects on myocardial oxygen demand, may cause peripheral ischemia, and has a proarrhythmic potential. Phenylephrine is contraindicated in patients with severe organic cardiac disease including coronary artery disease (e.g., angina, history of myocardial infarction, acute myocardial infarction), dilated cardiomyopathy, and/or cardiac arrhythmias associated with tachycardia (atrial fibrillation, atrial flutter, ventricular fibrillation, ventricular tachycardia). Phenylephrine also should not be used in patients with uncontrolled hypertension or peripheral vascular disease. Phenylephrine can cause a decrease in cardiac output, and should be used with extreme caution when given to patients with arteriosclerosis, coronary artery disease, or heart failure. In general, elderly patients are more susceptible than younger adults are to these effects. Although cardiovascular effects are uncommon with antihistamines such as promethazine, they should be used conservatively in patients with cardiac disease. The quinidine-like local anesthetic and anticholinergic effects of antihistamines may occasionally result in adverse cardiac effects. Opiate agonists, such as codeine, produce cholinergic side effects (by stimulating medullary vagal nuclei) causing bradycardia and vasovagal syncope, and induce the release of histamine, causing peripheral vasodilatation and orthostatic hypotension that may be problematic in patients with cardiac disease, hypotension, or hypovolemia.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products should be used with extreme caution in patients with significant pulmonary disease. Opiate agonists like codeine may cause respiratory depression. The anticholinergic activity of antihistamines like promethazine may result in thickened bronchial secretions in the respiratory tract thereby aggravating certain respiratory diseases. Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is contraindicated for use in the treatment of lower respiratory symptoms, including acute bronchial asthma. When possible, avoid use in patients with severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) (e.g., emphysema or bronchitis), chronic tobacco smoking, upper airway obstruction, or decreased respiratory drive. Avoid this drug combination in patients with a history of sleep apnea when possible, since codeine and promethazine are associated with CNS depressive effects. Although this drug combination should be avoided during acute bronchospasm or status asthmaticus, these actions may not preclude the use of this drug in all asthmatic or COPD patients. Avoid use in those patients that have previously experienced a worsening in respiratory status due to opiate agonist or antihistamine therapy.
 
Due to potential toxicities and increased side effects, codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products should be used cautiously in patients with hepatic disease. Codeine may accumulate leading to a prolonged duration of action in patients with decreased liver function. In acute situations, patients require close monitoring to avoid excessive toxicity. Also, promethazine is extensively metabolized in the liver. The metabolism of promethazine may be reduced in the presence of hepatic impairment. Patients with chronic hepatic disease may require less frequent dosing intervals.
 
Phenothiazine derivatives lower the seizure threshold through their effect on GABA; therefore, codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products should be avoided, if possible, in patients with a seizure disorder (seizures) or in those receiving anticonvulsants.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products are contraindicated for use in some children and adolescents due to the risk for fatal respiratory depression. Some manufacturers state that use is contraindicated in infants and children < 6 years of age ; other manufacturers contraindicate use in pediatric patients < 16 years of age . When used in combination with other respiratory depressants, promethazine has been associated with respiratory depression, sometimes fatal, in pediatric patients. In addition, postmarketing cases of respiratory depression, some fatal, have been reported in young children who received a wide range of weight-based doses of promethazine; promethazine is contraindicated in infants and children < 2 years. The exact mechanism of respiratory depression is not known. Excessively large doses of antihistamines, including promethazine, may cause sudden death in pediatric patients. Promethazine-associated extrapyramidal symptoms that may occur in children receiving promethazine-containing products may be confused with signs of undiagnosed primary disease; thus promethazine should be avoided in children whose signs or symptoms suggest the possibility of Reye's syndrome, hepatic encephalopathy, or other hepatic disease. In pediatric patients who are acutely ill associated with dehydration, there is an increased susceptibility to promethazine-associated dystonic reactions. Seizures and/or paradoxical CNS stimulation may also occur in this age group. In January 2008, the FDA issued a Public Health Advisory recommending that OTC cough and cold products not be used in infants and children less than 2 years. The FDA recommends that if parents and caregivers use cough and cold products in children greater than 2 years, labels should be read carefully, caution should be used when administering multiple products, and only measuring devices specifically designed for use with medications should be used. While some combination cough/cold products containing these ingredients are available by prescription only and are not necessarily under scrutiny by the FDA, clinicians should thoroughly assess each patient's use of similar products, both prescription and nonprescription, to avoid duplication of therapy and the potential for inadvertent overdose.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products are classified in FDA pregnancy risk category C. The pregnancy risk factor increases to category D if codeine-phenylephrine-promethazine is used for prolonged periods during pregnancy or in high doses close to term. There are no adequate studies in pregnant women; consider use during pregnancy only when the benefits of therapy outweigh the potential risks to the fetus. Due to the codeine component, neonates whose mothers have been taking this combination chronically may show some respiratory depression and/or withdrawal symptoms at birth or within a few days. Antihistamines like promethazine may cause transient EEG or behavioral changes in the newborn. If taken within two weeks of obstetric delivery, phenothiazines may inhibit platelet aggregation in the newborn. Systemic phenylephrine must be used only when the benefit to the mother outweighs the risk to the fetus during late pregnancy, labor or obstetric delivery; when used during this time phenylephrine can cause fetal anoxia and/or bradycardia due to increased uterine contractility or decreased uterine blood flow. Diagnostic pregnancy tests based on immunological reactions between HCG and anti-HCG may result in false-negative or false-positive interpretations in patients receiving promethazine.
 
According to the manufacturer, codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine should be used with caution during breast-feeding. It is not known if promethazine or phenylephrine are excreted in human milk. In general, antihistamines such as promethazine are not recommended for use during breast-feeding because they can induce a paradoxical CNS stimulation (e.g., transient EEG changes) in neonates. Inhibition of lactation may rarely occur. Codeine is distributed into breast milk at varying degrees depending upon the dose administered. Codeine is metabolized to morphine, which is also distributed into breast milk. Breast-fed infants of women who quickly metabolize codeine (ultra-rapid metabolizer) may ingest dangerous amounts of morphine. A healthy, 13-day-old breastfed baby died from a morphine overdose; the baby's blood morphine concentration was 70 ng/ml. The mother was taking codeine 30 mg and acetaminophen 500 mg tablets. The mother initially took 2 tablets every 12 hours for episiotomy pain, but she took half of this dose from day 2 to 14 because of somnolence and constipation. She stored her milk on day 10 because of poor neonatal feeding; the morphine concentration in the milk was 87 ng/ml. She was determined by genetic testing to be an ultra-rapid metabolizer of codeine (heterozygous for a CYP2D6*2A allele with CYP2D6*2x2 gene duplication). Ultra-rapid metabolizers have a specific CYP2D6 genotype and may change codeine to morphine more rapidly and completely than other people. Ultra-rapid metabolizers are more likely to have higher than normal blood and breast milk morphine concentrations after taking codeine. Ultra-rapid metabolism has only been reported as a problem with codeine, although ultra-rapid metabolism has the potential to affect other narcotics. Too much of any narcotic in breast milk can be fatal to a nursing infant. The estimated number of ultra-rapid metabolizers varies among different population groups from less than 1 per 100 people up to 28 per 100 people. Although a genetic test to identify ultra-metabolizers is available, the risk of having an adverse event when taking codeine is not known. The test result alone may not correctly predict if a mother's milk will have too much morphine if she ingests codeine. Consider the benefits of breast-feeding, the risk of potential infant drug exposure, and the risk of an untreated or inadequately treated condition. If a breast-feeding infant experiences an adverse effect related to a maternally ingested drug, healthcare providers are encouraged to report the adverse effect to the FDA.
 
When prescribing codeine, consider the race-related prevalence of altered codeine metabolism, including that of Asian patients, Black patients, Caucasian patients, Hispanic patients, and Middle Eastern patients. As codeine is metabolized to morphine via CYP2D6, inter-patient variances in CYP2D6 function may impact the analgesic or toxic effects of codeine. Patients with a deficiency of CYP2D6, those with low to non-functional CYP2D6 phenotypes, or those receiving inhibitors of this isoenzyme are poor metabolizers of codeine. Such patients may not adequately convert codeine to morphine and may not experience the expected analgesic response to codeine. Approximately 7—10% of Caucasians are poor CYP2D6 metabolizers. In contrast, some individuals may be ultra-rapid metabolizers due to a specific CYP2D6*2x2 genotype and are at increased risk of toxicity. The prevalence of this CYP2D6 phenotype varies widely and has been estimated at 0.5—1% in Chinese, Japanese, and Hispanics; 1—10% in Caucasians; 3—28% in black patients (3% in African Americans and 16—28% in North Africans and Ethiopians); and 16—28% of Arabs. Data are not available for other ethnic groups. Ultra-rapid metabolizers convert codeine to morphine more rapidly and completely than other people. Higher than expected serum morphine concentrations occur due to the rapid conversion, and serious toxicity such as extreme sedation, confusion, and apnea may occur. In addition, the breast milk of women who are ultra-rapid metabolizers taking codeine may also contain high concentrations of morphine, which may put a nursing infant at risk for toxic adverse effects (see breast-feeding Precaution).
 
Phenothiazines like promethazine may cause skin hyperpigmentation or photosensitivity. Patients should be warned to either avoid sunlight (UV) exposure or to use effective sunscreens (SPF 15+) on exposed areas of the body. Contact dermatitis is possible in predisposed individuals if they come in contact with liquid dosage forms of phenothiazines, avoid accidental exposure to the skin.
 
Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine contains a phenothiazine antihistamine (promethazine). Phenothiazines can lower the seizure threshold. Because of a potential increased risk of seizures, phenothiazines should not be used during intrathecal radiographic contrast administration. Discontinue this drug combination 48 hours before myelography and do not resume until at least 24 hours after the procedure.
 
Because codeine is an opiate agonist, codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine products are subject to substance abuse and psychologic dependence (i.e., drug addiction). Patients with a previous history of substance abuse may be at increased risk of relapse if treated with codeine. Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is not approved for the management of substance abuse.
 
Abrupt discontinuation of prolonged codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine therapy can result in withdrawal symptoms (see Adverse Reactions). Patients should be gradually tapered off this drug combination to avoid a withdrawal reaction. Generally, a 50% decrease every 1—2 days of the daily dose will prevent withdrawal symptoms in patients who have been receiving large daily doses of codeine.
 
Phenylephrine is contraindicated during the use of certain general anesthetics during surgery because increased cardiac irritability and myocardial sensitization to catecholamines may occur (see Drug Interactions).
 
Phenylephrine should be used with caution in patients with thyroid disease (i.e., hyperthyroidism or thyrotoxicosis) because these patients can be more sensitive to catecholamines; thyrotoxic or cardiotoxic symptoms can occur.
 
Because phenylephrine is an adrenergic agent, it should be given with caution to patients with diabetes mellitus. Promethazine has also been reported to increase blood glucose.
 
Phenylephrine-containing products should not be combined with MAOI therapy (see Drug Interactions).
  Contraindications And Precautions
    Headline
    Drug Interactions
    General Information
    The coadministration of certain medications may lead to harm and require avoidance or therapy modification; review all drug interactions prior to concomitant use of other medications.
    Headline
    Hypersensitivity
    General Information
    This medication is contraindicated in patients with a history of hypersensitivity to it or any of its components. Promethazine is contraindicated for use in patients with a known phenothiazine hypersensitivity. Cross-sensitivity may occur with other phenothiazines.
    Headline
    bone marrow suppression
    General Information
    Use codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine with caution in individuals with bone marrow suppression; leukopenia and agranulocytosis due to promethazine have been reported mostly in individuals who were receiving promethazine in association with other known marrow-toxic agents.
    Headline
    major psychiatric disorder, opioid overdose, risk of medication errors, substance abuse disorder
    General Information
    Opioids expose users to the risks of addiction, abuse, and misuse. Although the risk of addiction in any individual is unknown, it can occur in individuals appropriately prescribed an opioid. Addiction can occur at recommended dosages and if the medication is misused or abused. The risk of opioid-related overdose or overdose-related death is increased with higher doses, and this risk persists over the course of therapy. The potential for these risks should not prevent the proper management of pain in any given individual. Assess an individual's risk for opioid addiction, abuse, or misuse before prescribing an opioid, and monitor for the development of these behaviors or conditions. Risks are increased in individuals with a personal or family history of substance abuse disorder (including alcohol or drug abuse or addiction) or major psychiatric disorder (e.g., major depression). Individuals at increased risk may be prescribed opioids, but use in such individuals necessitates intensive counseling about the risks and proper use of the opioid along with frequent reevaluation for signs of addiction, abuse, and misuse. Opioids are sought for nonmedical use and are subject to diversion from legitimate prescribed use. Consider these risks when prescribing or dispensing opioids. Strategies to reduce these risks include prescribing the medication in the smallest appropriate quantity and educating about proper medication storage and disposal. Keep opioids out of the reach of children, others for whom the medication was not prescribed, and pets as accidental exposure or improper use may cause respiratory failure and a fatal overdose. Accidental exposure of even a single dose of an opioid, especially by younger individuals, can result in a fatal overdose. To reduce the risk of medication errors, ensure that dosing is communicated clearly and dispensed accurately. Discuss the importance of an opioid overdose reversal agent and the options for obtaining an opioid overdose reversal agent (e.g., prescription, over-the-counter, or as part of a community-based program), especially with individuals who are at increased risk of opioid overdose, such as those who are also using other CNS depressants, who have a history of opioid use disorder (OUD), who have experienced a previous opioid overdose, or who have household members or other close contacts at risk for accidental exposure or opioid overdose. Educate individuals and caregivers on how to recognize respiratory depression, and how to use an opioid overdose reversal agent for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose. Emphasize the importance of calling 911 or getting emergency medical help, even if an opioid overdose reversal agent is administered.
    Headline
    asthma exacerbation, cachexia, central sleep apnea, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, coadministration with certain medications, cor pulmonale, hypercapnia, hypoxia, respiratory depression, respiratory insufficiency, severe asthma
    General Information
    Codeine is contraindicated in individuals with significant respiratory depression and those with asthma exacerbation or severe asthma in an unmonitored setting or in the absence of resuscitative equipment. Avoid opioid coadministration with certain medications, specifically other CNS depressants when possible, as this significantly increases the risk for profound sedation, respiratory depression, coma, and death. Reserve concomitant prescribing of these medications for use in individuals for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate; if concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective dosages and minimum treatment durations needed. Monitor individuals closely for signs or symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Consider prescribing an opioid overdose reversal agent if concomitant use is warranted. Careful monitoring is required, particularly when CYP2D6 inhibitors or CYP3A4 inhibitors or inducers are used concomitantly with codeine. Concurrent use of a CYP3A4 inhibitor or discontinuation of a concurrently used CYP3A4 inducer may result in an increase in plasma codeine concentrations with subsequent greater metabolism by CYP2D6, resulting in greater morphine concentrations, which may increase the risk for respiratory depression, or discontinuation of a concomitantly used CYP2D6 inhibitor may result in a decrease in plasma codeine concentrations and an increase in plasma morphine concentrations, which may increase the risk for respiratory depression. Concurrent use of a CYP3A4 inducer or discontinuation of a CYP3A4 inhibitor may result in lower codeine concentrations, greater norcodeine concentrations, and less metabolism by CYP2D6 with resultant lower morphine concentrations, which may be associated with reduced efficacy and/or opioid withdrawal. Similarly, concurrent use of a CYP2D6 inhibitor may result in an increase in plasma codeine concentrations and a decrease in plasma morphine concentrations, which may be associated with reduced efficacy and/or opioid withdrawal. Individuals with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), cor pulmonale, respiratory insufficiency, hypoxia, hypercapnia, or preexisting respiratory depression are at increased risk of decreased respiratory drive even at recommended doses. Individuals with advanced age, cachexia, or debilitation are also at an increased risk for opioid-induced respiratory depression. Monitor such individuals closely, particularly when initiating and titrating the opioid; consider the use of non-opioid analgesics in these individuals. Carbon dioxide retention from opioid-induced respiratory depression may also worsen opioid sedating effects. Consider decreasing the opioid dosage in individuals with central sleep apnea (CSA). Opioids increase the risk of CSA and sleep-related hypoxemia in a dose-dependent fashion. Monitor individuals closely, especially within the first 24 to 72 hours of initiating therapy or when used in individuals at higher risk. An unresponsive cough should be reevaluated in 5 days or sooner for possible underlying pathology, such as foreign body or lower respiratory tract disease.
    Headline
    brain tumor, coma, head trauma, increased intracranial pressure, loss of consciousness
    General Information
    Avoid opioid use in individuals with loss of consciousness or coma; opioids may obscure the clinical course in an individual with a head trauma injury. Monitor individuals who may be susceptible to the intracranial effect of carbon dioxide retention (e.g., those with evidence of increased intracranial pressure or brain tumor) for signs of sedation and respiratory depression, particularly when initiating opioid therapy. Opioids may reduce respiratory drive and the resultant carbon dioxide retention can further increase intracranial pressure.
    Headline
    CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers
    General Information
    Do not use codeine in individuals who are CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers. Some individuals may be ultrarapid metabolizers due to a specific CYP2D6 genotype (gene duplications noted as *1/*1xN or *1/*2xN). Ultrarapid metabolizers convert codeine into morphine more rapidly and completely than other people. Higher than expected serum morphine concentrations occur due to the rapid conversion and serious toxicity including life-threatening or fatal respiratory depression may occur. The prevalence of this CYP2D6 phenotype varies widely and has been estimated at 1% to 10% in Whites, 3% to 4% in Blacks, 1% to 2% in East Asians (Chinese, Japanese, Korean), and may be greater than 10% in certain racial/ethnic groups (e.g., Oceanian, Northern African, Middle Eastern, Ashkenazi Jews, Puerto Rican). Approximately 7% to 10% of the White population lacks functional CYP2D6 activity.
    Headline
    activities requiring coordination and concentration, driving or operating machinery
    General Information
    Advise individuals against driving or operating machinery or participating in activities requiring coordination and concentration during opioid therapy unless they are tolerant to the effects of opioids and know how they will react to the medication. Codeine and promethazine cause drowsiness and impair the mental or physical abilities needed to perform potentially hazardous activities.
    Headline
    cardiovascular disease, hypovolemia, peripheral vascular disease, shock
    General Information
    Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is contraindicated in individuals with hypertension, severe coronary artery disease, or peripheral vascular disease (peripheral ischemia may result with risk of gangrene or thrombosis of compromised vascular beds). Phenylephrine increases peripheral vascular resistance and can produce cardiovascular effects in some individuals, such as dizziness, transient elevations in blood pressure, and may aggravate acute myocardial infarction, hypertension, peripheral vascular disease, or cardiac arrhythmias. Central nervous system stimulation with convulsions or cardiovascular collapse with accompanying low blood pressure has also been reported. Phenylephrine can cause a decrease in cardiac output. Use codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine with caution in individuals with other cardiovascular disease, including individuals with arteriosclerosis, elderly individuals, or individuals with cerebrovascular insufficiency (poor cerebral circulation). Avoid the use of opioids in individuals with circulatory shock. Opioids may cause vasodilation that can further reduce cardiac output and blood pressure. Monitor individuals whose ability to maintain blood pressure has been compromised by hypovolemia or concurrent administration of certain CNS depressant medications (e.g., phenothiazines, general anesthetics) for hypotension after opioid treatment initiation or dosage titration due to increased risk for severe hypotension.
    Headline
    acute pancreatitis, biliary tract disease, GI obstruction
    General Information
    Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is contraindicated in individuals with known or suspected GI obstruction, including paralytic ileus. Opioid agonists may obscure the diagnosis or clinical course in individuals with acute abdominal conditions. Opioids may cause spasm of the sphincter of Oddi or increases in serum amylase. Monitor individuals with biliary tract disease, including acute pancreatitis, for worsening symptoms.
    Headline
    urinary retention
    General Information
    Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is contraindicated in individuals with urinary retention.
    Headline
    hepatic failure
    General Information
    Use codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine with caution in individuals with hepatic impairment or hepatic failure. Monitor for adverse events such as respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension. No formal studies have been conducted with codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine in individuals with hepatic impairment, and the pharmacokinetics in this population are unknown.
    Headline
    renal failure
    General Information
    Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine should be used with caution in individuals with severe renal impairment or renal failure, and these individuals should be monitored closely for codeine-related side effects such as respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension. Clearance may be decreased and metabolites may accumulate in individuals with impaired renal function.
    Headline
    closed-angle glaucoma
    General Information
    Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is contraindicated in individuals with closed-angle glaucoma.
    Headline
    seizures
    General Information
    Codeine and/or promethazine may increase the frequency of seizures in individuals with a seizure disorder, and may increase the risk of seizures occurring in other clinical settings associated with seizures. Monitor individuals with a history of seizure disorders for worsened seizure control during codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine therapy.
    Headline
    diabetes mellitus
    General Information
    Use codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine with caution in people with diabetes mellitus as sympathomimetic agents such as phenylephrine may aggravate this condition.
    Headline
    adolescents, children, infants, major surgery, neonates, neuromuscular disease, obesity, obstructive sleep apnea, pulmonary disease
    General Information
    The Key Potentially Inappropriate Drugs in Pediatrics (KIDs) list includes codeine and promethazine as potentially inappropriate medications for use in neonates, infants, children, and adolescents and use should be avoided. If avoidance is not possible, use with caution and appropriate monitoring. Avoid codeine use in children and adolescents 12 to 18 years of age who have other risk factors for respiratory depression unless the benefits outweigh the risks. Risk factors include conditions associated with hypoventilation such as recent major surgery, obstructive sleep apnea, obesity, severe pulmonary disease, neuromuscular disease, and concomitant use of other medications that can cause respiratory depression. When prescribing codeine for adolescents, choose the lowest effective dose for the shortest period of time and inform individuals and caregivers of the risks and the signs of opioid overdose. Codeine metabolism is highly variable and unpredictable, particularly in children younger than 12 years; therapeutic response to recommended doses can range from lack of effect in poor metabolizers to fatality in ultrarapid metabolizers. Ultrarapid metabolizers are more likely to convert codeine to morphine quickly, leading to excessive morphine blood concentrations that can result in fatal respiratory depression. Because some children who are normal metabolizers can convert codeine to morphine at rates similar to ultra-rapid metabolizers, this concern extends to all pediatric individuals.
    Headline
    geriatric
    General Information
    Geriatric adults may have increased sensitivity to opioids. Use cautious dose selection for opioids in geriatric adults, usually starting at the low end of the dosing range, reflecting the greater frequency of decreased hepatic, renal, or cardiac function and concomitant disease or other medication therapy. Increase the opioid dosage slowly in geriatric adults and frequently reevaluate for signs of respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension. According to the Beers Criteria, opiate agonists are considered potentially inappropriate medications (PIMs) in geriatric patients with a history of falls or fractures and should be avoided in these populations, except in the setting of severe acute pain, since opiates can produce ataxia, impaired psychomotor function, syncope, and additional falls. If an opiate must be used, consider reducing the use of other CNS-active medications that increase the risk of falls and fractures, and implement strategies to reduce fall risk. First-generation phenothiazine-based antihistamines such as promethazine are considered PIMs for use in geriatric adults; avoid as they are highly anticholinergic, there is reduced clearance in advanced age, and there is a greater risk of anticholinergic effects compared to younger adults. Avoid use in older patients with the following conditions: delirium/high risk of delirium (new-onset or worsening delirium), dementia/cognitive impairment (drug-induced CNS effects), or lower urinary tract symptoms/benign prostatic hyperplasia in men (urinary retention or hesitancy). Avoid the use of promethazine in those with Parkinson's disease since the dopamine receptor-blocking effects of the drug have the potential to cause symptom exacerbation.
    Headline
    serious adverse effects
    General Information
    Opioids are associated with serious adverse effects, which may be life-threatening, including addiction, abuse, and misuse, respiratory depression, and neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome (NOWS). Accidental ingestion and concomitant use of certain other medications as well as ultra-rapid metabolism of codeine and other risk factors for life-threatening respiratory depression in children may increase the risk of these serious adverse effects.
    Headline
    labor, obstetric delivery, pregnancy
    General Information
    Available data on codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine use during pregnancy are insufficient to define the risk of major birth defects or miscarriage. Opioids cross the placenta. Published human data on the use of codeine during pregnancy are inconsistent. Some studies observed an increased risk of congenital malformations with codeine exposure while others did not. Specific malformations reported include respiratory malformations, spina bifida, and congenital heart defects. The majority of studies examining the use of promethazine in pregnancy do not identify an increased risk of congenital anomalies. In the few studies reporting an association, no consistent pattern of malformations was noted. Phenylephrine use during pregnancy can cause fetal tachycardia. Phenylephrine may also reduce blood flow to the placenta and the fetus. There is some evidence that its use may be associated with birth defects, particularly if used during early pregnancy. Evidence from case-control studies in human pregnancy indicates there may be an increased risk of gastroschisis, endocardial cushion defects, ear defects, and pyloric stenosis in newborns exposed in utero to phenylephrine, particularly in the first trimester. Opioids, such as codeine, can prolong labor and obstetric delivery by temporarily reducing the strength, duration, and frequency of uterine contractions. This effect is not consistent and may be offset by an increased rate of cervical dilatation, which may shorten labor. In addition, neonates exposed to opioids in utero may present with respiratory depression at birth. Because of this, an opioid antagonist (e.g., naloxone) must be available for reversal of opioid-induced respiratory depression in the neonate at delivery. Promethazine administered during pregnancy within 2 weeks of obstetric delivery may inhibit platelet aggregation in the newborn.
    Headline
    people who can cause pregnancy in others, people who may become pregnant, reproductive risk
    General Information
    Counsel people who may become pregnant and people who can cause pregnancy in others about the reproductive risk associated with opioids. Long-term use of opioids may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to hormonal changes that may manifest as a libido decrease, impotence, erectile dysfunction, amenorrhea, or infertility. The causal role of opioids in the clinical manifestations of hypogonadism is unknown. It is not known whether the effects on fertility are reversible.
    Headline
    neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero
    General Information
    Neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero may present with respiratory depression and psycho-physiologic effects at birth. Monitor exposed newborns for signs of excess sedation and respiratory depression. If necessary, administer an opioid antagonist (e.g., naloxone) to reverse the effects of opioid-induced respiratory depression. Long-term use of opioids during pregnancy can result in physical dependence in the exposed infant. Monitor newborns for symptoms of withdrawal and manage accordingly. Neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome may present as irritability, hyperactivity, abnormal sleep pattern, high-pitched cry, tremor, vomiting, diarrhea, and failure to gain weight. It can be life-threatening if not recognized and treated. Onset, duration, and severity of opioid withdrawal may vary based on the specific opioid used, duration of use, timing and amount of last maternal use, and rate of elimination by the infant. Additionally, promethazine given to a pregnant individual within 2 weeks of delivery may inhibit platelet aggregation in the newborn.
    Headline
    breast-feeding
    General Information
    Advise patients to avoid breast-feeding while taking codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine due to the potential for serious adverse reactions in the breastfed child, including excess sedation and respiratory depression. Codeine and its active metabolite, morphine, are present in human milk. In people with normal CYP2D6 activity, the amount of codeine present in milk is low and dose-dependent. However, CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers convert codeine to morphine more extensively, which may result in elevated morphine concentrations in milk and clinically significant opioid exposure in the breastfed child. If codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is used, monitor the breastfed child for increased sleepiness, poor feeding, breathing difficulty, and limpness. Excessive sedation in the lactating person may correlate with sedation in the breastfed child. Withdrawal symptoms may occur in the breastfed child when codeine-containing medications are stopped or breast-feeding is discontinued, particularly after chronic exposure. It is unknown if promethazine or phenylephrine are present in human milk. Direct oral administration of promethazine to pediatric patients is associated with respiratory depression, including fatalities. Theoretically, promethazine may lower basal prolactin secretion and may interfere with the establishment of lactation. Phenylephrine has poor oral absorption. Pharmacologically similar vasoconstrictors (i.e., pseudoephedrine) decrease milk production after oral use.

Pregnancy And Lactation
  Breastfeeding
    General Information
    Advise patients to avoid breast-feeding while taking codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine due to the potential for serious adverse reactions in the breastfed child, including excess sedation and respiratory depression. Interactions
Abiraterone: (Moderate) Concomitant use of codeine with abiraterone may increase codeine plasma concentrations, but decrease the plasma concentration of the active metabolite, morphine, resulting in reduced efficacy or symptoms of opioid withdrawal. It is recommended to avoid this combination when codeine is being used for cough. If coadministration is necessary, monitor patients closely at frequent intervals and consider a dosage increase of codeine until stable drug effects are achieved. Discontinuation of abiraterone could decrease codeine plasma concentrations and increase morphine plasma concentrations resulting in prolonged opioid adverse reactions, including hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death. If abiraterone is discontinued, monitor the patient carefully and consider reducing the opioid dosage if appropriate. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine, and by CYP3A4 to norcodeine; norcodeine does not have analgesic properties. Abiraterone is a moderate inhibitor of CYP2D6.
Acarbose: (Moderate) Sympathomimetic agents and adrenergic agonists tend to increase blood glucose concentrations when administered systemically. Monitor for loss of glycemic control when pseudoephedrine, phenylephrine, and other sympathomimetics are administered to patients taking antidiabetic agents. Epinephrine and other sympathomimetics, through stimulation of alpha- and beta- receptors, increase hepatic glucose production and glycogenolysis and inhibit insulin secretion. Also, adrenergic medications may decrease glucose uptake by muscle cells. For treatment of cold symptoms, nasal decongestants may be preferable for short term, limited use (1 to 3 days) as an alternative to systemic decongestants in patients taking medications for diabetes. (Minor) Phenothiazines, especially chlorpromazine, may increase blood glucose concentrations. Patients who are taking antidiabetic agents should monitor for worsening glycemic control when a phenothiazine is instituted.
Acebutolol: (Moderate) Monitor hemodynamic parameters and for loss of efficacy during concomitant sympathomimetic agent and beta-blocker use; dosage adjustments may be necessary. Concomitant use may antagonize the cardiovascular effects of either drug.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin, ASA; Caffeine: (Moderate) CNS-stimulating actions of caffeine can be additive with other CNS stimulants or psychostimulants like phenylephrine; caffeine should be avoided or used cautiously. Excessive caffeine ingestion (via medicines, supplements or beverages including coffee, green tea, other teas, guarana, colas) may contribute to side effects like nervousness, irritability, insomnia, or tremor.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin; diphenhydrAMINE: (Major) Reserve concomitant use of opioids and diphenhydramine for patients in whom alternate treatment options are inadequate. Limit dosages and durations to the minimum required and monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation. If concomitant use is necessary, consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose and monitor for signs of urinary retention or reduced gastric motility. Concomitant use can increase the risk of hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death as well as urinary retention and/or severe constipation, which may lead to paralytic ileus. (Moderate) Monitor for unusual drowsiness and sedation, urinary retention, and reduced gastric motility during coadministration of diphenhydramine and promethazine. Concomitant use may result in additive CNS depression or anticholinergic effects.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine: (Moderate) CNS-stimulating actions of caffeine can be additive with other CNS stimulants or psychostimulants like phenylephrine; caffeine should be avoided or used cautiously. Excessive caffeine ingestion (via medicines, supplements or beverages including coffee, green tea, other teas, guarana, colas) may contribute to side effects like nervousness, irritability, insomnia, or tremor.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Dihydrocodeine: (Major) Concomitant use of opioid agonists with promethazine may cause excessive sedation and somnolence. Avoid prescribing opioid cough medications in patients taking promethazine. Limit the use of opioid pain medications with promethazine to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, reduce the opioid dose by one-quarter to one-half; use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations needed to achieve the desired clinical effect. Educate patients about the risks and symptoms of excessive CNS depression. (Moderate) CNS-stimulating actions of caffeine can be additive with other CNS stimulants or psychostimulants like phenylephrine; caffeine should be avoided or used cautiously. Excessive caffeine ingestion (via medicines, supplements or beverages including coffee, green tea, other teas, guarana, colas) may contribute to side effects like nervousness, irritability, insomnia, or tremor.

Patients should be informed to read non-prescription cough and cold product labels carefully for additional interacting antihistamines. Acetaminophen; HYDROcodone: (Major) Concomitant use of hydrocodone with other CNS depressants may lead to hypotension, profound sedation, coma, respiratory depression and death. Prior to concurrent use of hydrocodone in patients taking a CNS depressant, assess the level of tolerance to CNS depression that has developed, the duration of use, and the patient's overall response to treatment. Consider the patient's use of alcohol or illicit drugs. Hydrocodone should be used in reduced dosages if used concurrently with a CNS depressant; initiate hydrocodone at 20 to 30% of the usual dosage in patients that are concurrently receiving another CNS depressant. Acetaminophen; oxyCODONE: (Major) Concomitant use of oxycodone with other opiate agonists may lead to additive respiratory and/or CNS depression. Hypotension, profound sedation, coma, respiratory depression, or death may occur. Prior to concurrent use of oxycodone in patients taking a CNS depressant, assess the level of tolerance to CNS depression that has developed, the duration of use, and the patient's overall response to treatment. Consider the patient's use of alcohol or illicit drugs. If a CNS depressant is used concurrently with oxycodone, a reduced dosage of oxycodone and/or the CNS depressant is recommended; use an initial dose of oxycodone at 1/3 to 1/2 the usual dosage. Limit the use of opioid pain medication with phenothiazines to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, reduce initial dosage and titrate to clinical response; use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations. Monitor for signs of hypotension after starting or titrating the dosage of oxycodone. There is an increased risk of severe hypotension in patients whose ability to maintain blood pressure has already been compromised by concurrent administration of phenothiazines.

Adagrasib: (Major) Concomitant use of adagrasib and promethazine increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary. (Moderate) Concomitant use of codeine with adagrasib may alter codeine plasma concentrations, resulting in an unpredictable effect such as reduced efficacy or symptoms of opioid withdrawal or prolonged opioid adverse reactions, including hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death. It is recommended to avoid this combination when codeine is being used for cough. If coadministration is necessary, monitor patients closely at frequent intervals and consider a dosage adjustment of codeine until stable drug effects are achieved. Discontinuation of adagrasib could alter codeine plasma concentrations, resulting in an unpredictable effect such as prolonged opioid adverse reactions or decreased opioid efficacy, and potentially lead to a withdrawal syndrome in those with physical dependence to codeine. If adagrasib is discontinued, monitor the patient carefully and consider adjusting the opioid dosage if appropriate. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine, and by CYP3A to norcodeine; norcodeine does not have analgesic properties. Adagrasib is a dual moderate CYP2D6 and strong CYP3A inhibitor. CYP3A inhibitors may increase codeine-related adverse effects while CYP2D6 inhibitors may reduce efficacy.

Opioids can reduce the efficacy of diuretics by inducing the release of antidiuretic hormone. Opioids may also potentiate orthostatic hypotension when given concomitantly with a thiazide diuretic. (Moderate) The cardiovascular effects of sympathomimetics may reduce the antihypertensive effects produced by diuretics. Well-controlled hypertensive patients receiving decongestant sympathomimetics at recommended doses do not appear at high risk for significant elevations in blood pressure, however, increased blood pressure has been reported in some patients.

Also, adrenergic medications may decrease glucose uptake by muscle cells. For treatment of cold symptoms, nasal decongestants may be preferable for short term, limited use (1 to 3 days) as an alternative to systemic decongestants in patients taking medications for diabetes. (Minor) Phenothiazines, especially chlorpromazine, may increase blood glucose concentrations. Hyperglycemia and glycosuria have been reported. Patients who are taking antidiabetic agents should monitor for worsening glycemic control when a phenothiazine is instituted. (Minor) Phenothiazines, especially chlorpromazine, may increase blood glucose concentrations. Hyperglycemia and glycosuria have been reported. When such drugs are administered to a patient receiving metformin, observe the patient closely for loss of blood glucose control. When such drugs are withdrawn from a patient receiving metformin, observe the patient closely for hypoglycemia.
Alogliptin; Pioglitazone: (Moderate) Sympathomimetic agents tend to increase blood glucose concentrations when administered systemically. Monitor for loss of glycemic control when sympathomimetics are administered to patients taking dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4) inhibitors. Sympathomimetics, through stimulation of alpha- and beta- receptors, increase hepatic glucose production and glycogenolysis and inhibit insulin secretion. Also, adrenergic medications may decrease glucose uptake by muscle cells. For treatment of cold symptoms, nasal decongestants may be preferable for short term, limited use (1 to 3 days) as an alternative to systemic decongestants in patients taking medications for diabetes. (Moderate) Sympathomimetic agents tend to increase blood glucose concentrations when administered systemically. Monitor for loss of glycemic control when sympathomimetics are administered to patients taking thiazolidinediones. Sympathomimetics, through stimulation of alpha- and beta- receptors, increase hepatic glucose production and glycogenolysis and inhibit insulin secretion. Also, adrenergic medications may decrease glucose uptake by muscle cells. For treatment of cold symptoms, nasal decongestants may be preferable for short term, limited use (1 to 3 days) as an alternative to systemic decongestants in patients taking medications for diabetes. (Minor) Phenothiazines, especially chlorpromazine, may increase blood glucose concentrations. Hyperglycemia and glycosuria have been reported. Patients who are taking antidiabetic agents should monitor for worsening glycemic control when a phenothiazine is instituted.
Alosetron: (Major) Patients taking medications that decrease GI motility may be at greater risk for serious complications from alosetron, like constipation, via a pharmacodynamic interaction. Constipation is the most frequently reported adverse effect with alosetron. Alosetron, if used with drugs such as opiate agonists, may seriously worsen constipation, leading to events such as GI obstruction/impaction or paralytic ileus. (Moderate) Alosetron, if combined with drugs that possess anticholinergic properties like phenothiazines, may seriously worsen constipation, leading to events such as GI obstruction/impaction or paralytic ileus.
Alpha-blockers: (Major) Sympathomimetics can antagonize the effects of antihypertensives such as alpha-blockers when administered concomitantly.
Alpha-glucosidase Inhibitors: (Moderate) Sympathomimetic agents and adrenergic agonists tend to increase blood glucose concentrations when administered systemically. Monitor for loss of glycemic control when pseudoephedrine, phenylephrine, and other sympathomimetics are administered to patients taking antidiabetic agents. Epinephrine and other sympathomimetics, through stimulation of alpha- and beta- receptors, increase hepatic glucose production and glycogenolysis and inhibit insulin secretion. Also, adrenergic medications may decrease glucose uptake by muscle cells. For treatment of cold symptoms, nasal decongestants may be preferable for short term, limited use (1 to 3 days) as an alternative to systemic decongestants in patients taking medications for diabetes. (Minor) Phenothiazines, especially chlorpromazine, may increase blood glucose concentrations. Patients who are taking antidiabetic agents should monitor for worsening glycemic control when a phenothiazine is instituted.
ALPRAZolam: (Major) Concomitant use of opiate agonists with benzodiazepines may cause respiratory depression, hypotension, profound sedation, and death. Limit the use of opiate pain medications with benzodiazepines to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations needed to achieve the desired clinical effect. If an opiate agonist is initiated in a patient taking a benzodiazepine, use a lower initial dose of the opiate and titrate to clinical response. If a benzodiazepine is prescribed for an indication other than epilepsy in a patient taking an opiate agonist, use a lower initial dose of the benzodiazepine and titrate to clinical response. Educate patients about the risks and symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Avoid prescribing opiate cough medications in patients taking benzodiazepines. (Major) Limit dosage and duration of benzodiazepines during concomitant phenothiazine use and monitor for unusual drowsiness and sedation due to the risk for additive CNS depression. (Moderate) The therapeutic effect of phenylephrine may be decreased in patients receiving benzodiazepines. Monitor patients for decreased pressor effect if these agents are administered concomitantly.
Alvimopan: (Moderate) Patients should not take alvimopan if they have received therapeutic doses of opiate agonists for more than seven consecutive days immediately before initiation of alvimopan therapy. Patients recently exposed to opioids are expected to be more sensitive to the effects of mu-opioid receptor antagonists and may experience adverse effects localized to the gastrointestinal tract such as abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea.
Amantadine: (Moderate) Although the mechanism of amantadine is not clear, it may potentiate the actions of dopamine. Since phenothiazines are dopamine antagonists, these drugs are best avoided when possible in patients with Parkinson's disease who require amantadine therapy. Also, the anticholinergic effects of phenothiazines can be additive to those of amantadine.
Ambrisentan: (Major) Sympathomimetics can antagonize the effects of vasodilators when administered concomitantly. Patients should be monitored for reduced efficacy if taking ambrisentan with a sympathomimetic.
Amide local anesthetics: (Moderate) The use of these drugs together must be approached with caution. Although commonly used together for additive analgesic effects, the patient must be monitored for respiratory depression, hypotension, and excessive sedation due to additive effects on the CNS and blood pressure. In rare instances, serious morbidity and mortality has occurred. Limit the use of opiate pain medications with local anesthetics to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations needed to achieve the desired clinical effect. The use of the local anesthetic may allow for the use a lower initial dose of the opiate and then the doses can be titrated to proper clinical response. Educate patients about the risks and symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation.
Amifampridine: (Major) Carefully consider the need for concomitant treatment with phenothiazines and amifampridine, as coadministration may increase the risk of seizures. Consider an alternative to the phenothiazine. If coadministration occurs, closely monitor patients for seizure activity. Seizures have been observed in patients without a history of seizures taking amifampridine at recommended doses. Phenothiazines may lower seizure threshold and should be used with caution with concomitant medications which may also affect seizure threshold.
Amikacin: (Minor) Antiemetics, like promethazine, should be used carefully with aminoglycosides because they can mask symptoms of ototoxicity (e.g., nausea secondary to vertigo). These agents block the histamine or acetylcholine response that causes nausea due to vestibular (inner ear) emetic stimuli such as motion.
aMILoride: (Moderate) Monitor for decreased diuretic efficacy and additive orthostatic hypotension when amiloride is administered with codeine. Adjustments to diuretic therapy may be needed in some patients. The efficacy of diuretics may be reduced due to opioid-induced release of antidiuretic hormone. (Moderate) The cardiovascular effects of sympathomimetics may reduce the antihypertensive effects produced by diuretics. Well-controlled hypertensive patients receiving decongestant sympathomimetics at recommended doses do not appear at high risk for significant elevations in blood pressure, however, increased blood pressure has been reported in some patients.
aMILoride; hydroCHLOROthiazide, HCTZ: (Moderate) Monitor blood pressure during concomitant thiazide diuretic and phenothiazine use. Thiazide diuretics may potentiate the orthostatic hypotension that may occur with phenothiazines. (Moderate) Monitor for decreased diuretic efficacy and additive orthostatic hypotension when amiloride is administered with codeine. Adjustments to diuretic therapy may be needed in some patients. The efficacy of diuretics may be reduced due to opioid-induced release of antidiuretic hormone. (Moderate) Monitor for signs of diminished diuresis and/or effects on blood pressure during coadministration of a thiazide diuretic and codeine; increase the dosage of the thiazide diuretic as needed. Opioids can reduce the efficacy of diuretics by inducing the release of antidiuretic hormone. Opioids may also potentiate orthostatic hypotension when given concomitantly with a thiazide diuretic. (Moderate) The cardiovascular effects of sympathomimetics may reduce the antihypertensive effects produced by diuretics. Well-controlled hypertensive patients receiving decongestant sympathomimetics at recommended doses do not appear at high risk for significant elevations in blood pressure, however, increased blood pressure has been reported in some patients.
Aminoglycosides: (Minor) Antiemetics, like promethazine, should be used carefully with aminoglycosides because they can mask symptoms of ototoxicity (e.g., nausea secondary to vertigo). These agents block the histamine or acetylcholine response that causes nausea due to vestibular (inner ear) emetic stimuli such as motion.
Aminolevulinic Acid: (Moderate) Phenothiazines may increase the photosensitizing effects of photosensitizing agents used in photodynamic therapy. Patients should limit ultra-violet exposure.
Amiodarone: (Major) Concomitant use of amiodarone and promethazine increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary. Due to the extremely long half-life of amiodarone, a drug interaction is possible for days to weeks after drug discontinuation. (Moderate) Concomitant use of codeine with amiodarone may alter codeine plasma concentrations, resulting in an unpredictable effect such as reduced efficacy or symptoms of opioid withdrawal or prolonged opioid adverse reactions, including hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death. It is recommended to avoid this combination when codeine is being used for cough. If coadministration is necessary, monitor patients closely at frequent intervals and consider a dosage adjustment of codeine until stable drug effects are achieved. Discontinuation of amiodarone could alter codeine plasma concentrations, resulting in an unpredictable effect such as prolonged opioid adverse reactions or decreased opioid efficacy, and potentially lead to a withdrawal syndrome in those with physical dependence to codeine. If amiodarone is discontinued, monitor the patient carefully and consider adjusting the opioid dosage if appropriate. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine and by CYP3A to norcodeine (norcodeine does not have analgesic properties); amiodarone is a dual weak CYP2D6 and weak CYP3A inhibitor. CYP3A inhibitors may increase codeine-related adverse effects while CYP2D6 inhibitors may reduce efficacy. (Moderate) Use phenylephrine with caution in patients receiving amiodarone. Amiodarone possesses alpha-adrenergic blocking properties and can directly counteract the effects of phenylephrine. Phenylephrine also can block the effects of amiodarone. Monitor patients for decreased pressor effect and decreased amiodarone activity if these agents are administered concomitantly.
Amisulpride: (Major) Concomitant use of promethazine and amisulpride increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Amitriptyline: (Major) Concomitant use of opioid agonists with tricyclic antidepressants may cause excessive sedation and somnolence. Limit the use of opioid pain medications with tricyclic antidepressants to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, reduce initial dosage and titrate to clinical response; use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations. Also monitor patients for the emergence of serotonin syndrome. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs. The concomitant use of opioids with other drugs that affect the serotonergic neurotransmitter system has resulted in serotonin syndrome. Avoid prescribing opioid cough medication in patients taking tricyclic antidepressants. (Major) Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) may markedly enhance the pressor response to parenteral direct-acting sympathomimetic agents such as norepinephrine and, to a lesser extent, epinephrine and phenylephrine. TCAs inhibit norepinephrine reuptake in adrenergic neurons, resulting in increased stimulation of adrenergic receptors. Clinically, the patient might experience hypertension, headache, tremor, palpitations, chest pain, or irregular heartbeat. (Moderate) Monitor for unusual drowsiness or excess sedation and for signs or symptoms of anticholinergic toxicity during concomitant promethazine and tricyclic antidepressant use. Concomitant use may result in additive CNS depression or anticholinergic adverse effects.
amLODIPine: (Moderate) Monitor for an increase in codeine-related adverse reactions including sedation and respiratory depression if coadministration with amlodipine is necessary; adjust the dose of codeine if necessary. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine, and by CYP3A4 to norcodeine; norcodeine does not have analgesic properties. Amlodipine is a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor. Concomitant use may result in an increase in codeine plasma concentrations, resulting in greater metabolism by CYP2D6 and increased morphine concentrations. (Moderate) Phenylephrine's cardiovascular effects may reduce the antihypertensive effects of calcium-channel blockers. Well-controlled hypertensive patients receiving decongestant sympathomimetics at recommended doses do not appear to be at high risk for significant elevations in blood pressure; however, increased blood pressure (especially systolic hypertension) has been reported in some patients.

amLODIPine; Celecoxib: (Moderate) Concomitant use of codeine with celecoxib may increase codeine plasma concentrations, but decrease the plasma concentration of the active metabolite, morphine, resulting in reduced efficacy or symptoms of opioid withdrawal. It is recommended to avoid this combination when codeine is being used for cough. If coadministration is necessary, monitor patients closely at frequent intervals and consider a dosage increase of codeine until stable drug effects are achieved. Discontinuation of celecoxib could decrease codeine plasma concentrations and increase morphine plasma concentrations resulting in prolonged opioid adverse reactions, including hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death. If celecoxib is discontinued, monitor the patient carefully and consider reducing the opioid dosage if appropriate. Codeine is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6 to morphine, and by CYP3A4 to norcodeine; norcodeine does not have analgesic properties. Celecoxib is an inhibitor of CYP2D6.
