    agranulocytosis, aplastic anemia, eosinophilia, fever, hemolytic anemia, leukopenia, neutropenia, pancytopenia, platelet dysfunction, purpura, thrombocytopenia
    General Information
    Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine combinations contain the phenothiazine antihistamine promethazine. Leukopenia, agranulocytosis, thrombocytopenia, and thrombocytopenic purpura have been reported with promethazine. Promethazine administered to a pregnant woman within 2 weeks of delivery may inhibit platelet aggregation (platelet dysfunction) in the newborn. Leukopenia, including agranulocytosis, is the most common hematologic disturbance that has been reported during phenothiazine administration. Agranulocytosis has occurred primarily in conjunction with other agents known to cause bone marrow toxicity. Other hematologic abnormalities that have been associated with phenothiazine therapy include eosinophilia, neutropenia, pancytopenia, aplastic anemia, and hemolytic anemia. It is advisable to instruct patients to promptly report fever or other symptoms or signs of infection if such symptoms or signs occur.

    Headline
    neuroleptic malignant syndrome
    General Information
    Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) is a rare but serious reaction that may occur in patients receiving phenothiazines, including promethazine. NMS is characterized by hyperpyrexia, severe extrapyramidal dysfunction, alterations in consciousness, altered mental status, and autonomic instability (tachycardia, high or low blood pressure, diaphoresis). Increased serum creatine phosphokinase (CPK), acute renal failure, and leukocytosis have also occurred. NMS does not appear to be dose-related. Severe cases have resulted in death 3 to 30 days after the onset of the syndrome. Several predisposing factors may contribute to the development of NMS including heat stress, physical exhaustion, dehydration, and organic brain disease. NMS occurs more frequently in young men. Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine combinations should be immediately discontinued, and appropriate supportive therapy initiated as soon as symptoms of NMS are discovered.

    Headline
    contact dermatitis, photosensitivity, rash (unspecified), skin hyperpigmentation
    General Information
    Prolonged therapy with phenothiazines can lead to rash (unspecified) or skin hyperpigmentation. Skin hyperpigmentation is generally restricted to areas of the body exposed to sunlight. Rarely, photosensitivity can result and patients should be warned either to keep out of the sun or to use effective sunscreens (SPF 15+) on exposed areas of the body.     Headline
    neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome, neonatal respiratory depression, physiological dependence, psychological dependence, withdrawal
    General Information
    As with other opiate agonists, use of codeine presents the potential for abuse. Although the true prevalence of addiction to opioids in chronic pain patients is unknown, estimates from multiple studies indicate that it may range from 0% to 50% and 0% to 7.7% in chronic non-malignant pain and cancer pain, respectively. Physiological dependence, however, will occur during chronic codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine therapy as evidenced by a withdrawal syndrome occurring after abrupt discontinuation of the drug. Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is indicated for short-term use; therefore, addiction and physiological dependence are not expected to occur. Symptoms of withdrawal include nausea and diarrhea, coughing, lacrimation, yawning, sneezing, rhinorrhea, profuse sweating, twitching muscles, abdominal and muscles pain/cramps, hot and cold flashes, and piloerection. Elevations in body temperature, respiratory rate, heart rate, and blood pressure may also occur. Without treatment most symptoms resolve within 5 to 14 days; however, there appears to be a secondary or chronic abstinence syndrome which may last 2 to 6 months, characterized by insomnia, irritability and muscle aches. The shorter the onset and duration of action of the opiate agonist, the greater the intensity and rapidity of onset of withdrawal symptoms. Routine use of opioid agonists by an expectant mother can lead to neonatal respiratory depression and a neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome. Withdrawal symptoms in a newborn occur 1 to 4 days after birth and include generalized tremors, hypertonicity with any form of tactile stimuli, hyperalertness, sleeplessness, excessive crying, vomiting, diarrhea, yawning, and pyrexia. It is important to differentiate physiological dependence, the onset of a withdrawal syndrome upon abrupt discontinuation of the drug, and psychological dependence. Psychological dependence is a behavioral syndrome characterized by drug craving, overwhelming concern with acquisition of the drug and other drug-related behaviors such as drug selling and seeking the drug from multiple sources.

    Headline
    hyperthermia, hypothermia
    General Information
    Promethazine is a medication with antihistaminic properties and a phenothiazine-based chemical structure. Phenothiazines have been reported to disrupt the body's ability to reduce core body temperature presumably through effects in the hypothalamus, and they predispose patients to hyperthermia. Patients receiving codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine should be advised of conditions that contribute to an elevation in core body temperature (e.g., strenuous exercise, ambient temperature increase, or dehydration). A less frequently described alteration in thermoregulatory processes reported with phenothiazines is hypothermia. Thermoregulation is multi-factorial; however, the dopaminergic system appears to have a primary role, and serotonin may also have modulatory activity (5-HT2a receptors). Most cases of hypothermia have occurred in conjunction with other potential precipitating factors such as hypothyroidism, sepsis, organic brain injury, or environmental temperature. Hypothermia appears to occur more frequently during initiation of phenothiazine therapy or after dose increases.

    Headline
    adrenocortical insufficiency
    General Information
    Cases of adrenocortical insufficiency have been reported with opioid use, more often following more than 1 month of use. Presentation of adrenocortical insufficiency may include non-specific symptoms and signs including nausea, vomiting, anorexia, fatigue, weakness, dizziness, and low blood pressure. If adrenal insufficiency is suspected, confirm the diagnosis with diagnostic testing as soon as possible. If adrenal insufficiency is diagnosed, treat with physiologic replacement doses of corticosteroids. Wean the individual off of the opioid to allow adrenal function to recover and continue corticosteroid treatment until adrenal function recovers. Other opioids may be tried, as some cases reported use of a different opioid without recurrence of adrenal insufficiency. The information available does not identify any particular opioids as being more likely to be associated with adrenal insufficiency. Opioids inhibit the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), cortisol, and luteinizing hormone (LH). Monitor individuals for symptoms of opioid-induced endocrinopathy, particularly those receiving a daily dose equivalent to 100 mg or more of morphine. Individuals should seek immediate medical attention if they experience symptoms of adrenocortical insufficiency. Chronic opioid use may lead to symptoms of hypogonadism, resulting from changes in the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis. Males with signs or symptoms of androgen deficiency should undergo evaluation.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      adrenocortical insufficiency
      Severity

    Headline
    laboratory test interference
    General Information
    Laboratory test interference may occur when administering codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine. Because opioid agonists may increase biliary tract pressure, with resultant increase in plasma amylase or lipase levels, determination of these enzyme levels may be unreliable for 24 hours after administration of a dose of codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine. Promethazine may interfere with some pregnancy tests; diagnostic pregnancy tests based on immunological reactions between HCG and anti-HCG may result in false-negative or false-positive interpretations. An increase in blood glucose has been reported in individuals receiving promethazine; glucose tolerance test results may be affected.
    Headline
    amenorrhea, gonadal suppression, impotence (erectile dysfunction), infertility, libido decrease
    General Information
    Opioids may interfere with the endocrine system by inhibiting the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), cortisol, and luteinizing hormone (LH), and by stimulating secretion of prolactin, growth hormone (GH), insulin, and glucagon. Chronic use of opioids may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to hormonal changes that may manifest as hypogonadism (gonadal suppression). Although the exact causal role of opioids in the clinical manifestations of hypogonadism is unknown, patients could experience libido decrease, impotence (erectile dysfunction), amenorrhea, or infertility. Other various medical, physical, lifestyle, and psychological stressors may influence gonadal hormone concentrations; these stressors have not been adequately controlled for in clinical studies with opioids. Patients presenting with signs or symptoms of androgen deficiency should undergo laboratory evaluation.

Description
  Text
  Codeine, phenylephrine, and promethazine are used together in an oral preparation to treat upper respiratory congestion, cough, and related symptoms. Phenylephrine is a sympathomimetic agent that causes vasoconstriction and decreases nasal and sinus congestion. Promethazine is a phenothiazine-related antihistamine that dries the nasal and sinus passages. Codeine is an opiate agent with antitussive activity. Codeine is a prodrug that is hepatically metabolized to morphine. Codeine metabolism is highly variable and unpredictable, particularly in children younger than 12 years; therapeutic response to recommended doses can range from lack of effect in poor metabolizers to fatality in ultra-rapid metabolizers. Opioid cough and cold medicines that contain codeine are not indicated in pediatric patients under 18 years of age. These products are contraindicated in neonates, infants and children less than 12 years of age; risks of opioid-containing cough and cold medicines outweigh their benefits in patients younger than 18 years. Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine is also contraindicated in adolescent pediatric patients 12 to 18 years of age after tonsillectomy or adenoidectomy.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  The cumulative actions of codeine in combination with phenylephrine and promethazine result in the relief of cough, nasal congestion, and associated symptoms.
Codeine: Codeine is a mu-opiate receptor agonist. Opiate receptors include mu, kappa kappa, and delta, which have been reclassified by an International Union of Pharmacology subcommittee as OP1 (delta), OP2 (kappa), and OP3 (mu). The antitussive effects of codeine are mediated through direct action on receptors in the cough center of the medulla. Codeine also has a drying effect on the respiratory tract and increases the viscosity of bronchial secretions. Cough suppression can be achieved at lower doses than those required to produce analgesia. Codeine also has a drying effect on the respiratory tract and increases the viscosity of respiratory secretions. Hypotension is possibly due to an increase in histamine release and/or depression of the vasomotor center in the medulla. Induction of nausea and vomiting possibly occurs from direct stimulation of the vestibular system and/or the chemoreceptor trigger zone.
Phenylephrine: Phenylephrine possesses both direct and indirect sympathomimetic effects, primarily as a postsynaptic alpha-adrenergic agonist, producing potent vasoconstriction. An indirect effect due to the release of norepinephrine plays a small role in the overall action of phenylephrine. Phenylephrine does not stimulate beta-2 adrenergic receptors in the bronchi or peripheral blood vessels or beta-1 adrenergic receptors of the heart. Phenylephrine increases resistance and, to a lesser extent, decreases capacitance of blood vessels. Following oral administration, constriction of blood vessels leads to reduced blood flow to the nose, decreased amount of blood in the sinusoid vessels, and decreased mucosal edema, which relieves nasal congestion.
Promethazine: The predominant action of promethazine is antagonism of H1-receptors. Like other sedating antihistamines, promethazine competes with free histamine for binding at H1-receptor sites. Histamine receptors in the GI tract, uterus, large blood vessels, and bronchial muscle are blocked. The relief of motion sickness and nausea/vomiting appear to be related to central anticholinergic actions and may implicate activity on the medullary chemoreceptor trigger zone. Other CNS receptor sites can also be affected, since promethazine is believed to indirectly reduce stimuli to the brainstem reticular system. Sedation is significant at concentrations achieved from therapeutic dosages. Mild antitussive activity has been attributed to promethazine, but this effect probably results from anticholinergic and sedative actions. Although promethazine is classified as a phenothiazine, its ability to antagonize dopamine is approximately one-tenth that of chlorpromazine. However, promethazine, like other phenothiazines, may produce adverse reactions such as dystonia or hyperthermia.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Codeine: Codeine is well-absorbed after oral administration with maximum plasma concentrations occurring 60 minutes after a dose. The presence of a high-fat, high-calorie meal did not significantly impact the pharmacokinetics. Clinical antitussive effects generally last 4 to 6 hours.
Phenylephrine: Phenylephrine is irregularly absorbed from and readily metabolized in the GI tract. The bioavailability of phenylephrine is about 38%. After oral administration, nasal decongestion occurs within 15 to 20 minutes and persists for up to 4 hours.
Promethazine: Promethazine is well absorbed after oral administration. Clinical effects are apparent within 20 minutes after oral administration and generally last 4 to 6 hours, although they may persist as long as 12 hours.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    The clearance of both promethazine and codeine may be decreased in patients with hepatic impairment; both drugs are extensively metabolized by the liver. No formal studies of this combination in hepatic impairment are available; patients with hepatic impairment should be monitored closely for codeine and/or promethazine toxicity such as respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension. Dose-response data in patients with liver cirrhosis suggest some hepatic impaired patients may have a decreased response to phenylephrine.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    The clearance of codeine and its active metabolite morphine are decreased in the presence of renal impairment. Codeine pharmacokinetics may be altered in patients with renal failure. Clearance may be decreased and the metabolites (e.g., morphine) may accumulate to much higher plasma levels in patients with renal failure as compared to patients with normal renal function. Patients with end stage renal disease (ESRD) on hemodialysis may also have increased responsiveness to phenylephrine at the usual dose.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    CYP2D6 Ultra-rapid Metabolizers
Some individuals may be ultra-rapid metabolizers due to a specific CYP2D6 genotype (gene duplications noted as *1/*1xN or *1/*2xN). Ultra-rapid metabolizers of CYP2D6 convert codeine into morphine more rapidly and completely than others. Higher than expected serum morphine concentrations occur due to the rapid conversion and serious toxicity, including life-threatening or fatal respiratory depression, may occur. The prevalence of this CYP2D6 phenotype varies widely and has been estimated at 1% to 10% in White patients, 3% to 4% in Black patients (African Americans), 1% to 2% in East Asian patients (Chinese, Japanese, Korean), and may be greater than 10% in certain racial/ethnic groups (e.g., Oceanian, Northern African, Middle Eastern, Ashkenazi Jews, Puerto Rican).
 
CYP2D6 Poor Metabolizers
Poor metabolizers of CYP2D6 or those receiving inhibitors of this isoenzyme may not adequately convert codeine to its active metabolite, morphine, which may decrease the expected therapeutic effect. Approximately 7% to 10% of the White patient population lacks functional CYP2D6 activity.
  Text
  Codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine combinations are administered orally.
Codeine: Codeine has been reported to have an apparent volume of distribution of approximately 3 to 6 L/kg, indicating extensive distribution of the drug into tissues. Codeine has low plasma protein binding (7% to 25%) to plasma proteins. Codeine passes the blood brain barrier and the placental barrier. Small amounts of codeine and its metabolite, morphine, are transferred to human breast milk. Codeine is metabolized by conjugation with glucuronic acid to codeine-6-glucuronide (about 70 to 80%), by O-demethylation to morphine (about 5 to 10%), and by N-demethylation to norcodeine (about 10%). UDP-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) 2B7 and UGT2B4 are the major enzymes mediating glucuronidation of codeine to codeine-6-glucuronide. Whether codeine-6-glucuronide has pharmacological activity is unknown. CYP2D6 is the major enzyme responsible for conversion of codeine to morphine and CYP3A4 is the major enzyme mediating conversion of codeine to norcodeine. Morphine and norcodeine are further metabolized by conjugation with glucuronic acid. The glucuronide metabolites of morphine are morphine-3-glucuronide (M3G) and morphine-6-glucuronide (M6G). Morphine and its M6 glucuronide conjugate are pharmacologically active. Norcodeine and the M3 glucuronide conjugate of morphine are not considered pharmacologically active. Approximately 90% of the total dose of codeine is excreted through the kidneys, of which approximately 10% is unchanged codeine. Plasma half-lives of codeine and its metabolites have been reported to be approximately 3 hours.
Phenylephrine: The volume of distribution at steady state is approximately 340 L, suggesting high distribution into organ and tissue compartments. Penetration into the blood brain barrier is minimal. Phenylephrine is metabolized primarily by monoamine oxidase (MAO) and sulfotransferase in the intestinal wall and liver. There are 2 major metabolites, m-hydroxymandelic acid and sulfate conjugates, that are not pharmacologically active. Phenylephrine and metabolites are excreted mainly in the urine within 48 hours. The mean elimination half-life of phenylephrine is around 2.5 hours.
Promethazine: Promethazine is highly protein-bound (80% to 93%). It is widely distributed in body tissues and fluids, and it crosses the placenta and is excreted into breast milk. Promethazine is metabolized by the liver to a variety of inactive metabolites, including promethazine sulfoxide, N-demethylpromethazine, and other glucuronides. The elimination half-life is 10 to 14 hours, with excretion of metabolites in the urine and the feces. The sulfoxides of promethazine and N-demethylpromethazine are the predominant metabolites appearing in the urine.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP2D6, CYP3A4
The effects of concomitant use or discontinuation of CYP3A4 inducers, CYP3A4 inhibitors, or CYP2D6 inhibitors with codeine are complex. Use of any such drugs with codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine requires careful consideration of the effects on the parent drug, codeine, and the active metabolite, morphine. Coadministration of codeine; phenylephrine; promethazine with CYP3A4 or CYP2D6 inhibitors or CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided. If not avoidable, consider the potential for respiratory depression/opioid toxicity or the potential for decreased efficacy and/or withdrawal.
 
The use of CYP3A4 inhibitors may result in an increase in codeine plasma concentrations with subsequently greater metabolism by CYP2D6, resulting in increased morphine concentrations, which could increase or prolong adverse reactions and may cause potentially fatal respiratory depression. Discontinuation of such an inhibitor may result in lower codeine levels, greater norcodeine levels, and less metabolism via 2D6 with resultant lower morphine concentrations. This may be associated with a decrease in efficacy, and in some patients, may result in signs and symptoms of opioid withdrawal.
 
The use of a CYP3A4 inducer may result in lower codeine levels, greater norcodeine levels, and less metabolism via 2D6 with resultant lower morphine concentrations. This may be associated with a decrease in efficacy, and in some patients, may result in signs and symptoms of opioid withdrawal. Conversely, if such an inducer is discontinued, increased codeine plasma concentrations may occur with subsequently greater metabolism by 2D6, resulting in greater morphine levels, which could increase or prolong adverse reactions and may cause potentially fatal respiratory depression.
 
Finally, the use of a CYP2D6 inhibitor may result in an increase in codeine plasma concentrations and a decrease in morphine plasma concentrations, which could result in an analgesic efficacy reduction or symptoms of opioid withdrawal. Discontinuation of a CYP2D6 inhibitor may result in a decrease in codeine plasma concentration and an increase in morphine plasma concentration which could increase or prolong adverse reactions and may cause potentially fatal respiratory depression.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Liquid Formulations
      Description
      Advise patients/caregivers to use a calibrated spoon or other measuring device. Do not to overfill.
Rinse the measuring device with water after each use.
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Administer orally with a full glass of water. May be taken with food or milk to minimize GI irritation.
Do not administer more frequently than recommended or prescribed as severe, possibly fatal, respiratory depression may occur.
An unresponsive cough should be reevaluated in 5 days or sooner for possible underlying pathology, such as foreign body or lower respiratory tract disease.

Maximum Dosage Limits
  Age Groups

    Adults
    Max Dosage
    30 mL/24 hours PO.
    Name

    Max Dosage
    30 mL/24 hours PO.
    Name
    Adolescents
    Max Dosage
    Safety and efficacy have not been established.
    Name

    Max Dosage
    12 years: Safety and efficacy have not been established.
Less than 12 years: Use is contraindicated.
    Name
    Infants
    Max Dosage
    Use is contraindicated.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Dosage adjustment may be required in patients with hepatic impairment but no specific recommendations are available. Elimination of both codeine and promethazine will likely be decreased. Use with caution and monitor closely for codeine toxicity (e.g., respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension). Dose-response data in patients with liver cirrhosis suggest some hepatic impaired patients may have a decreased response to phenylephrine.
  Renal Impairment
  Specific dose adjustment recommendations are not available; however, decreased elimination of codeine and metabolites (e.g., morphine) can occur. Use with caution in patients with severe renal impairment or renal failure, and monitor closely for signs of codeine toxicity (respiratory depression, sedation, and hypotension). Patients with end stage renal disease (ESRD) on hemodialysis may also have increased responsiveness to phenylephrine at the usual dose.
